== 2026-08-23 (#92) -- the row that was taking the system's gesture

*Attempted:* issue #92 -- "trying to switch the application by swiping is easily interpreted as
mark as played. Add a margin around the podcast and episode list."

The report names the remedy, and the remedy is right, but the reason is worth writing down because
it decides the number: the rows on S1 and S2 were `fillMaxWidth`, so the node carrying the click --
and, on S2, the `SwipeToDismissBox` -- started at x = 0. Every horizontal gesture the system reserves
for itself at the screen edges was therefore also live app surface. Losing that race costs a `PLAY`
action on a shared server, not a mis-tap.

*Built:* `ListGutter` (16 dp) and `gutterFor()` in `:core:ui`, and `listGutterPadding()`, which
insets a list by `max(the device's own systemGestures inset, 16 dp)`. A fixed 16 would have been the
obvious read of the issue and is not enough -- plenty of devices reserve 24, and the leftover 8 dp
strip is exactly where the collision happens. Both ``LazyColumn``s take it as `contentPadding`, and the
rows give up their own horizontal padding in exchange, so on a 16 dp device the content grid does not
move at all: the row shrinks to meet the gutter rather than the content shifting inward.

The part that is easy to get wrong is what is _inside_ the list: the S2 month headers and S1's
summary line had `padding(horizontal = RowPadding)` of their own, which after the change would sit a
gutter's width deeper than the rows they belong to. Both are vertical-only now, and `docs/UI.md` §17
says so, because that is precisely the drift that section exists to catch.

*Tests.* `ListGutterTest` covers the arithmetic (a device reserving 0, 8, 16, 24). The one that
matters is `ListGestureGutterTest`: it renders each screen at `w360dp` and asserts the row's own
bounds start and end at least a gutter from the edges. Confirmed to fail before the fix -- both cases,
with the row at x = 0 -- which is what makes it a regression test rather than a description.

`./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green: *803 tests, 0 failures, 3 skipped.*

*Not done, deliberately:* S7's activity list gets no gutter. Its rows are not swipeable, so they
are not in the race, and widening the change to "every list" would have been scope the issue did not
ask for.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (#91) -- the download was not the problem

*Attempted:* issue #91 -- "once a download is started, the UI becomes unresponsive. It is also
briefly unresponsive when swiping to mark as played."

Two symptoms, one cause, and the cause is not in `:core:download` at all. Every list view model ends
`.stateIn(viewModelScope, …)`, and `viewModelScope` collects on `Dispatchers.Main.immediate`. With no
`flowOn` anywhere in the project, *every mapping above that `stateIn` was main-thread work* -- each
row projected into an `EpisodeUi`, then the whole list grouped into month sections -- re-run on every
emission of every source being combined.

That is why the two symptoms are one bug. Live byte progress is one of the combined sources and ticks
once a second for the entire download; a ledger write is another, and that is the swipe. ADR 0027
measured 9,500 episodes reaching the screen in 82--132 ms; the mistake in reading that number as
"fast enough" was assuming it happens once, at screen open. It was happening every second.

*A second cost, independent and possibly larger.* S2's item body did:

[,kotlin]
----
val header = state.sections.firstOrNull { it.firstIndex == items.indexOf(episode) }
----

`items.indexOf(episode)` is a linear scan comparing a data class field by field -- for every _visible_
row, on every recomposition. Twenty rows against a few thousand episodes is tens of thousands of
string comparisons per frame, to decide whether to draw a month label. It is now a map built once per
`sections` change and keyed by the section's first index.

*Built:* an injected `projectionContext` (default `Dispatchers.Default`) and `.flowOn` on the three
view models that project lists and re-emit during a download -- S2, S1 and S7. The other three
(`EpisodeDetail`, `ErrorLog`, `Settings`) project one item or a short list and are not in the download
path; widening the change to them would have been scope this issue did not ask for, and `docs/UI.md`
§B0.8 now states the rule so the next list screen gets it right.

*The test that did not work, which is the interesting part.* The first version asserted that the
state stays at its initial value until the projection dispatcher is advanced. It passed -- and passed
just as well with `flowOn` deleted, because the fake sources are asynchronous anyway, so the state is
empty at that moment either way. A test that cannot fail is not evidence. The version that ships
counts dispatches to the injected context with a `CountingDispatcher`: without `flowOn` the context is
never used at all, so the count is zero. Verified failing before the fix, passing after.

`./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green: *805 tests, 0 failures, 3 skipped.*

*Honest limits.* This is a structural fix verified by structural tests, on the JVM. Nobody has
watched the app during a real download since the change -- the claim "the UI no longer freezes" is
reasoning plus a dispatch assertion, not an observation. The device set (`docs/dev-environment.md` §6)
is where that would be settled, and it has not been run for this.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (#90) -- a decision you can find again

*Attempted:* issue #90 -- a history of actions, "if an episode is marked by mistake and the undo
notification is missed, it is impossible to track which should be reverted."

The word doing the work is *track*. Nothing was ever unrecoverable: _Mark as unplayed_ has existed
since `docs/decisions/0024`, and S2's filters can reach any episode. What was missing is knowing
_which_ episode -- a swipe holds its decision for five seconds (§12.3) and is then silent, and an
episode that leaves the _To decide_ filter leaves the screen. The feature is therefore a list, not a
new capability.

*Asked before building*, because it decides how much of the app moves: a group inside S7, a ninth
screen, or widening S7's existing _recently downloaded_ group. The author chose the group in S7,
which is what the issue itself pointed at ("similar to the download history"). It costs no new route,
no new ViewModel, and no schema change -- `EpisodeLedgerRow` already carries `state` and `actionedAt`.

*Built:* `EpisodeListDao.observeRecentActions(limit)` -- the `episodes ⨝ episode_ledger` join,
every decided state, newest first, `LIMIT` in SQL. Three decisions worth recording:

* *Every decided state, not just `SKIPPED`.* "What did I just do?" does not distinguish a wrong
_Download_ from a wrong _Mark as played_.
* *In-flight states excluded.* `QUEUED`/`DOWNLOADING`/`ERROR` are already the three groups above
this one; including them would put the same episode on one screen twice.
* *A join, not the ledger alone.* S7's delivered group reads ledger rows because it renders a
filename, and that keeps it correct for an unsubscribed feed. This group has to name an episode the
user can _recognise_, so it joins -- and an episode whose cache was pruned by an unsubscribe drops
out of it. The ledger row survives, which is the part that matters (CLAUDE.md §11).

The row says *Played*, not `SKIPPED` -- the vocabulary rule §1 sets -- and `HANDLED_REMOTELY` renders
as _Handled elsewhere_, because presenting another client's decision as "you played this" would have
the app asserting something it does not know. A row already `UNPLAYED` gets no button.

*The cost nobody plans for:* six test fakes implement `EpisodeListRepository` across five modules,
so one port method meant six overrides. Four are `flowOf(emptyList())` with a comment saying why
that is honest rather than lazy; the two that back tests of this feature return real data.

`./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green: *812 tests, 0 failures, 3 skipped.*

*Honest limits.* Verified on the JVM only -- the group renders correctly under Robolectric and the
write goes through the same `TriageWriter` as S2's, but nobody has swiped an episode on a phone,
opened S7 and taken it back. Fifty entries is a guess at "enough to find a mis-swipe in": there is no
paging and no _load more_, so a decision older than the last fifty is not reachable here.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (emulator) -- the fix that only broke where the tests could not look

*Attempted:* run the three issue fixes (#90, #91, #92) on the in-container emulator, since all
three had shipped with "verified on the JVM, nobody has looked at it" attached.

=== What the run proved

* *#90 end to end, which is what the emulator is good for.* Marked _Folge 2_ as played from S2's
overflow, waited out the undo window, opened S7: the *RECENT ACTIONS* group listed it as
_Played · just now · Silo Stories_, newest first, above the two older decisions. Tapped _Mark as
unplayed_; `episode_ledger` went `SKIPPED → UNPLAYED` with `syncedToServer = 0`, the row intact.
The row then re-rendered as _Marked unplayed · just now_ *with no button*, which is the one rule
a JVM test asserts abstractly and a screen can still get wrong.
* *`driver.py smoke` green* on the full stack: seed → SAF grant → mark played → real 100 KB
download over HTTPS → ID3 rewrite → SAF delivery → both ledger rows.
* *#92's gutter is 30 dp on this device, not 16.* `WindowInsets.systemGestures` reports 78 px at
density 420. Taking the max rather than hardcoding 16 dp was the right call, and this is the first
evidence for it: a fixed 16 would have left a 14 dp strip of live swipe surface under the system's
own gesture.

=== The defect, and why the tests were blind to it

That same 30 dp is what broke the layout. The lists moved out to the gutter; the filter chips and
banners, which sit _outside_ the list and keep their own `RowPadding`, stayed on the 16 dp grid. The
result is a visible step between the chip row and the first row under it -- measured 42 px against
78 px.

*Robolectric reports a zero gesture inset*, so in every JVM test the gutter is exactly the 16 dp
floor and the two grids coincide. The suite could not have caught this, and the PR I had already
written said "the content grid does not move at all", which is true only on a device that reserves
16 dp or less. The emulator was the first place the claim met a number that was not 16.

Fixed with `chromeGutterFor(gutter)` -- what a chrome row needs _added_ to its own padding to reach
the gutter, zero when they agree. First attempt put it on the container that also holds the list,
which applied the gutter twice and pushed the rows out to 43 dp; caught by measuring rather than by
looking, which is the argument for `dump` over screenshots. It belongs on the #92 branch, so the
stack was rebased: `fix/92` → `fix/91` → `feat/90`, all three re-verified after.

=== Still not settled, and the phone is the only place it can be

* *#91's actual smoothness.* The structural fix is in and the app works during a download, but
this emulator is software-rendered under nested virtualisation -- frame timing here measures the
emulator, not the fix.
* *#92's original symptom.* The headless AVD has no gesture navigation to compete with, so the
30 dp inset is a number, not a resolved collision.

=== A tooling papercut worth knowing

`driver.py smoke` is not idempotent: it taps _Folge 1_ by name, and its own previous run leaves that
episode with a ledger row, which hides it under the default _To decide_ filter. It needs a `reset`
first, every time after the first. Not worth a rebase of four branches today -- noted here, and the
second run's file came out as `… (2).mp3`, which is the collision suffixing behaving correctly
against the file the first run had already written.

`./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green: *814 tests, 0 failures, 3 skipped.*

'''

== 2026-08-23 (device) -- the phone answered the two questions the JVM could not

*Attempted:* verify #90, #91 and #92 on the author's Pixel 10a (Android 17) over wireless
debugging, against the real `cloud.drehtuer.net` account -- the three fixes had all shipped with
"verified on the JVM, nobody has looked at it" attached.

=== Getting on the phone

`adb pair` failed with a protocol fault, and it did not matter: the adb key from the August sessions
is still trusted, so `adb connect` alone worked. The *pairing port is not the connect port* --
§9.4 says so and I still tried the pairing port first. The connect port came from the documented
scan, since mDNS does not cross into the container.

Two environmental failures cost a run each, and neither was a code problem:

. *The phone was dozing with the lockscreen up.* Every Compose test failed with _"No compose
hierarchies found in the app"_, which is what the test rule says when the activity cannot reach
the foreground. Worth knowing because it reads exactly like a broken app.
. *A 30-second screen timeout* would have re-created that mid-run, so it was raised to 30 minutes
for the session and put back afterwards.

=== The set

61 tests, in two halves. 39 across `core/datastore`, `core/download`, `core/ui`,
`feature/episodes` and `feature/settings` -- all green. Then ``:app``'s 22, which is where the
interesting part is: *the 6 `SafDownloadTargetInstrumentedTest` cases and
`DownloadPipelineInstrumentedTest` only run if a folder grant and refreshed episodes exist*, and
`device-test.sh` destroys the first by reinstalling. Installing *only the test APK* over an
already-granted app is what let them run: `am instrument` directly, no Gradle, no reinstall. All 7
passed, and the pipeline test left the ledger byte-identical to the baseline -- it cleans up after
itself, which the JVM cannot show.

One failure along the way was a *flake*: `everyFilterChipIsReachableAtTheDeviceWidth` died with
"Activity has been destroyed already" and passed on re-run. That is the test most likely to be broken
by #92's gutter, so its passing is a positive result rather than an absence of a negative.

=== #92, settled

The gutter resolves to *30 dp on this phone* -- the same as the emulator, and the reason taking
`max(systemGestures, 16 dp)` rather than hardcoding 16 was right. Chrome and rows both land at
x = 78 px, so the alignment fix holds on real hardware with real data.

The gesture itself, which no emulator could answer: *a swipe starting at x = 10 px navigated back*
-- the system took it -- with no triage and no snackbar. A swipe starting inside the content area still
triages, and its undo still writes nothing. That is the whole issue, closed on the device that
reported it. Caveat: these are injected events, not a finger.

=== #91, measured at last

`dumpsys gfxinfo` during a *real download of a real episode*, scrolling the list throughout:
*266 frames, 8 janky (3.01 %)*, 50th 6 ms, 90th 11 ms, 95th 15 ms. Against a 2,468-episode cache
and 2,917 ledger rows. The claim in the PR was reasoning plus a dispatch assertion; this is the
number.

*What it is not: an A/B.* Nobody measured the pre-fix build on this phone, so the honest statement
is "smooth now", not "n times smoother". The before-number would need the `fix/92` build installed
and another real download.

=== What the account cost, and how it was returned

The author asked for any _mark as played_ to be reversed. The baseline was clean and unusually easy
to diff: *2,917 rows, every one `HANDLED_REMOTELY`, nothing unsynced* -- so anything else was mine.

The download wrote one row (`DOWNLOADED`, then `syncedToServer = 1`, so `DOWNLOAD` and `PLAY` reached
the server). It was reversed through *S7's own _Mark as unplayed_* -- which is #90 being used for
its actual purpose on real data, not a demo -- leaving `UNPLAYED`, synced, which other clients read as
unread (`docs/decisions/0022`). The row itself stays, because the row is the dedup authority and has
to outlive the decision (`docs/decisions/0024`). "Reversed" therefore means _the episode reads as
unread everywhere_, not _the record is gone_, and those are deliberately different things.

The 63 MB file remains in the author's `Podcasts/PseudoPod/` folder. Podsilo does not delete files
(CLAUDE.md §1), and an agent deleting one because it created it is a rule this project should not
start bending; it is theirs to remove.

=== A network problem that was a typo

Mid-session the author could not connect: _"cannot reach address"_. The container reached
`cloud.drehtuer.net` in 76 ms, the phone pinged it in 35 ms, and the VPN tunnel's 1283-byte MTU
looked like a promising culprit -- which is where I started speculating instead of reading. The app's
own error log had it in one line:

----
AUTH ×3 · Connecting to Nextcloud failed: UNREACHABLE
host 'cloud drehtuer.net' rejected before contacting anything
----

A *space instead of a dot*. `docs/decisions/0019` paid for itself a second time, and the lesson is
the same one as last time: read the error log before theorising about the network.

It leaves a real observation, unfixed and unrecorded elsewhere: a *malformed host reports as
`UNREACHABLE`*, the same word a genuine network failure gets. The `detail` line distinguishes it,
the headline does not, and that is what sent two of us to look at a VPN. Same family as issue #40.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (address check) -- the typo that borrowed a network failure's words

*Attempted:* the author's request after the device session -- _"add a check if the url is valid,
that would make the problem immediately visible"_. The problem being a space in
`cloud drehtuer.net`, which cost a session of looking at a VPN's MTU.

*What was already there, and why it did not help.* `hostProblem()` has always rejected a blank or
whitespace-containing address before contacting anything. The fault was not the missing check -- it
was that both cases returned `ConnectError.UNREACHABLE`, whose message is _"Can't reach that address.
Check the spelling and your network."_ The app therefore said the one thing guaranteed to send a
reader to their network, about a string that never left the device. The error log's `detail` line
did say _"rejected before contacting anything"_, but the headline is what people read.

*Built:* two typed errors that name the fault -- `ADDRESS_HAS_SPACE` and `ADDRESS_INVALID` -- and
validation on *every keystroke* rather than only on submit, which is what "immediately visible"
asks for. The space message names the character and shows the shape the address should have; a
generic "invalid address" would be true and useless, since the whole difficulty is that a space is
invisible in a URL. An empty field stays silent: not started is not a mistake.

Parsing is `java.net.URI`, not a regex: hostnames, IPv4 literals, bracketed IPv6, ports and a
subdirectory install are all legal, and the set of things that are _not_ is much harder to write down
than to delegate. The check stays permissive -- a single-label LAN name like `nextcloud` is a real
setup -- because its job is to make a typo visible, not to have opinions about the author's network.

*A pre-existing bug fell out of writing the tests.* `hostProblem("https://")` returned _valid_.
`normaliseHost` strips trailing slashes first, so `https://` becomes `https:`, which no longer matches
the scheme test and gets re-prefixed into `+https://https:+` -- an address whose host is `https`. The
test asserting it was invalid failed, I assumed the test was wrong, and checking the actual
`java.net.URI` behaviour in a scratch file showed it was the code. The validator now rejects a
scheme with nothing after it before parsing.

`./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green: *819 tests, 0 failures, 3 skipped.*

*Verified on the phone* (same session, after the PR was written and while it still said this had
not been done). Typed into S5 on the Pixel 10a, with the app already connected:

|===
| Typed | Shown under the field

| `cloud drehtuer.net`
| _Addresses can't contain spaces -- check for a stray one (cloud.example.org, not cloud example.org)._

| `://`
| _That doesn't look like a server address. It should read like cloud.example.org._

| `cloud.drehtuer.net`
| nothing -- cleared
|===

The half that is the actual requirement: *the message appeared while typing*, with
_Request authorization_ never touched, and the error log gained *zero* new `AUTH` entries. The old
behaviour needed a submit to say anything, wrote a log line, and called it `UNREACHABLE`. The dialog
was cancelled without connecting; the instance, the account and the ledger were unchanged afterwards.

*A mis-tap worth recording, because it was mine and it was avoidable.* The first attempt tapped
into the _author's own screen_: the phone was awake and unlocked, so I tapped -- and the foreground
app was Claude, not Podsilo, because the author was reading the conversation on the same device. It
opened a menu, which was dismissed with one Back press, but the lesson is that "awake and unlocked"
and "showing the app I think it is" are different questions, and only the second one licenses a tap.
The retry ran through a focus-guarded driver that re-reads `mCurrentFocus` immediately before every
tap and aborts rather than tapping. It fired the check about a dozen times and never had to abort,
which is the point: the guard costs nothing and the mistake cost an apology.

*Integration checked after the merge* (all six PRs plus dependabot's Compose BOM bump, 2026.06.01
→ 2026.08.00, which none of this work was built against): `ktlintCheck detekt test` on merged `main`
is green at *819 tests, 0 failures, 3 skipped*.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (0.7.0) -- a release about taking a decision back

Cut after the device session. Three of the four user-visible changes came from issues the author
filed while using 0.6.0 on the phone, which is the first time that has been the shape of a release.

* *S7 lists what you decided* (#90). The gap it fills is not a missing capability -- _Mark as
unplayed_ has existed since `docs/decisions/0024` -- but a missing _list_: a swipe is silent five
seconds after it happens, and an episode that leaves _To decide_ leaves the screen. Recent actions
is the finding, and it was used for exactly that during the device session, to reverse the one
write the testing made.
* *An edge swipe goes to the system again* (#92). Rows were `fillMaxWidth`, so the node carrying
the swipe started at x = 0 and competed with back and app-switch. The gutter is
`max(the device's own gesture inset, 16 dp)` -- 30 dp on the author's phone, which is why a
hardcoded 16 would not have been enough.
* *The list no longer freezes during a download* (#91). Nothing to do with the downloader: every
list view model projected its rows on the main thread, re-run on every emission of every combined
source -- and live byte progress ticks once a second for the length of a download.
* *A bad address names itself* (#98), instead of borrowing the words of a network failure.

*What this release is verified by*, in the order the evidence is worth anything: 819 JVM tests; 61
device tests on the Pixel 10a with no skips, including the six SAF cases that only run when a folder
grant survives the install; and the two UI fixes driven by hand on the phone that reported them --
an edge swipe reaching the system, and 3.01 % janky frames during a real download against a
2,468-episode library.

*What it is not.* #91 has no before-number: nobody measured the pre-fix build on that phone, so the
honest claim is "smooth now", not a factor. The release build was verified locally before the tag was
pushed, because this repository's immutable releases mean a tag spent on a broken build is a version
number lost -- which is how v0.5.0 went.


'''

== 2026-08-23 (asciidoc) — two converters, and the one that ate a chapter

*Attempted:* the author's request — convert the documentation to AsciiDoc, add asciidoctor and a
linter to the dev container, and say which site generator suits AsciiDoc on GitHub.

=== What could not convert

Three files stay Markdown, and none of it is preference. `.claude/CLAUDE.md` is loaded by Claude Code
by that exact name, so converting it silently unloads the project's own instructions.
`.claude/skills/run-podsilo/SKILL.md` is required by the skill format. And GitHub's community-health
detection accepts only `.md`/`.txt`, so `SECURITY.adoc` would drop out of the Security tab — verified
rather than assumed, against an open feature request with no sign of landing.

=== Converter one: pandoc, and 234 deleted headings

Chosen first for a bad reason: it is in apt, and the Dockerfile header says distro packages only. That
is a packaging constraint, not a technical argument, and I never put the alternative in front of the
author.

Pandoc emits an explicit `[[anchor]]` above every heading. *AsciiDoc ids may not begin with a digit*,
and every numbered section (`1. System context`) and dated journal entry produced exactly that. An
invalid anchor does not warn on its own — *it takes its heading out of the rendered document with
it*. 234 of them. It surfaced only because a downstream heading then failed the "section title out of
sequence" check, which `--failure-level=WARN` turns into a non-zero exit.

=== Converter two: kramdoc, and the chapter that vanished

The author asked why pandoc, which was the right question. `kramdoc` — the Asciidoctor project's own
Markdown converter — emits no explicit anchors, no `[cols=",,,"]` noise, no `'''''`, and lints clean
untouched. It also treats mermaid as a *diagram* language by default, producing the `[mermaid]` block
that GitHub does not render; `--diagram-languages=plantuml` demotes it to a source block, which is the
spelling GitHub does read.

Then it silently folded *the whole of Part C of `UI.adoc`* — six headings, its tables, its lists,
roughly 400 lines — into a single paragraph. The cause is four bare `<tag>` tokens in the prose:
`<podcast>`, `<name>`. kramdown parses those as raw HTML and swallows content until a closing tag.
*The linter did not catch this*, because one enormous paragraph is perfectly valid AsciiDoc. The
link check caught it: seven cross-references into Part C stopped resolving.

Two lessons, and the second is the one worth keeping:

. *Those four tokens were already broken in Markdown.* GitHub passes bare `<podcast>` through as raw
  HTML, and a browser renders an unknown empty element as nothing at all — so that content
  description has been rendering as `"cover art for "` for as long as it has existed. Backticking
  them is a fix, not an accommodation.
. *A linter that cannot see the failure is not verification.* What caught it was comparing structure
  across the conversion — heading counts before and after, and every cross-reference resolved against
  the ids actually generated. That check now runs over every file and is the reason this entry can
  claim nothing was lost: 83 headings in `UI.md`, 83 in `UI.adoc`.

=== What the link check found

Every internal xref was resolved against generated ids. 35 needed repointing, because Asciidoctor
renders an em dash differently from GitHub-Markdown (`b3-s2episode-list` against `b3-s2--episode-list`)
and `8.1` as `8-1` where Markdown dropped the dot. Two were *already broken before any of this*: a
link to `#6-s3--episode-detail-sheet` for a section renamed to "Episode detail (full screen)", and one
to section 10 for a section numbered 11. Nothing had ever checked.

=== The journal keeps its `.md` references

612 references across 201 files were repointed, including the KDoc citations in Kotlin comments. This
file was excluded: 7a9545a settled that a log is not edited to match the present, and an extension is
no different from a rename. The header note says so.

`asciidoctor --failure-level=WARN` is CI's eighth check, beside yamllint and shellcheck.


'''

== 2026-08-23 (antora) — a layout that made every comment too long

*Attempted:* the author's second thought — Antora rather than a plain Asciidoctor build — and the
restructure that goes with it.

=== What Antora costs

Two things, and both were visible before starting rather than discovered afterwards:

* *Pages must live at `docs/modules/ROOT/pages/`.* `antora.yml` and `modules/` have to be siblings,
  and every page belongs to a family directory inside a module. There is no flatter arrangement.
* *Page links are `xref:` macros, which GitHub's file view does not resolve.* An
  `xref:architecture.adoc[]` renders there as a link to `architecture.html`, which does not exist.
  The built site is the reading surface now; browsing the repository still shows prose and diagrams,
  but its cross-references are dead. That directly contradicts the criterion the author started from
  — "compatible with GitHub" — and is the price of the navigation, search and single start page.

=== The cost that was not visible

Rewriting 629 source citations to the new path pushed KDoc comment lines past detekt's 120-character
limit in six files, and `MaxLineLength` failed the build. `docs/modules/ROOT/pages/UI.adoc §12.3` is
28 characters of directory to say "UI".

The fix is not to rewrap 600 comments: it is to stop putting a path in them. There is one module and
one page per name, so `UI.adoc §12.3` and `decisions/0013` are unambiguous, and the path lives in one
place — this document and the README — rather than in every comment that cites a section. Citations
in the pages themselves are module-relative for the same reason.

=== What was verified

* `antora --fetch antora-playbook.yml` builds 22 pages with *no unresolved xrefs* — checked by
  looking for Antora's `xref page unresolved` class in the output rather than trusting a silent exit.
* Every `xref:`/`link:` target across the pages and the README resolves to a file that exists. That
  check caught a converter bug worth knowing: *kramdoc rewrites `.md` link targets to `.adoc`*, so
  the README's links to `SECURITY.md`, `.claude/CLAUDE.md` and `SKILL.md` — the three files that
  deliberately stay Markdown — had been silently repointed at files that do not exist.
* `./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green.

=== Left undone, deliberately

*Mermaid does not render on the site.* GitHub draws `[source,mermaid]` natively; Antora's stock UI
shows it as source. Fixing it needs either `asciidoctor-kroki` — which means a Kroki server, public
or self-hosted — or a supplemental UI that loads `mermaid.js` in the reader's browser. Both are
choices about what the site depends on, not implementation details, so neither was made here.

*Nothing publishes the site.* The playbook writes `build/site/`; no workflow deploys it. GitHub Pages
cannot build AsciiDoc itself, so publishing means an Actions job that runs Antora and uploads the
artifact.


'''

== 2026-08-23 (kroki) — the diagrams that had never rendered anywhere

*Attempted:* a Kroki server in the dev container, so the site draws the Mermaid diagrams Antora's
stock UI was showing as source.

=== Having it both ways

GitHub renders Mermaid inside `.adoc` only from `[source,mermaid]`. `asciidoctor-kroki` renders only
`[mermaid]`. Writing either one breaks the other, and these diagrams are read in both places.

The way out is that Antora hands extensions the aggregated content before Asciidoctor sees it:
`antora-extensions/mermaid-source-blocks.js` rewrites the declaration there, so the files on disk keep
GitHub's spelling and Kroki gets its own. Twenty-odd lines, and nothing on disk changes.

Kroki itself is self-hosted beside opodsync rather than the public `kroki.io` — which would have
worked with no compose service at all, and would have posted every architecture and UI diagram in this
repository to a third party on each build. They are not secret. Sending them anyway, when a container
costs one `docker compose up`, is a habit worth not forming.

=== Two failures, and a wrong turn between them

Four of the 26 diagrams came back `400`. They were the four longest, which pointed at size, and I
promptly tested the theory the wrong way: `curl -X POST` without a content type sends form-encoded
data, and Kroki mangles that, so my "reproduction" failed for a reason that had nothing to do with
the build. Adding `-H 'Content-Type: text/plain'` made the same diagram render, which looked like the
answer and was not.

The actual cause was in the build's own error, which I had skimmed: `GET
http://localhost:8000/mermaid/svg/eNptU8Fu…`. The extension encodes the diagram into a *GET URL* by
default, and Kroki rejects anything past 4000 characters. `kroki-http-method: post` fixes it, and the
lesson is the ordinary one — the stack trace named the request; I went and invented a different one.

Two diagrams still failed after that, and those were real: *Mermaid treats `;` as a statement
separator*, so an unquoted semicolon truncates a message and the rest fails to parse. Both had
carried one for months, and *both had almost certainly never rendered on GitHub either* — same
grammar. Nobody noticed, because a diagram that fails to parse on GitHub is a quiet blank box,
whereas a diagram that fails to render through Kroki is a build warning. The site build is what
surfaced them.

All 26 render now, and the build is warning-free.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (pages) — publishing the site, and a dependency that was never in the image

*Attempted:* deploy the Antora site to GitHub Pages, now that the repository's Pages source is set to
*GitHub Actions* rather than a branch.

=== The rebuild found the bug for me

The dev container had just been rebuilt, and the first `antora --fetch antora-playbook.yml` died with
`Cannot find module 'asciidoctor-kroki'`. Yesterday's Kroki work had installed that package by hand
and never written it into `.devcontainer/Dockerfile` — so the *only* machine on which the diagrams
had ever rendered was the one running container that had the ad-hoc install. A rebuild was always
going to reveal it; a CI job would have revealed it the same way, on the day it was needed.

Worth naming as a pattern, because it is not specific to npm: *a tool used to produce a committed
result belongs in the image in the same change as the result.* The commit before this one described a
Kroki pipeline that no fresh checkout could actually run.

Pinning it was its own trap. The current release is `asciidoctor-kroki@1.0.1`, it installs cleanly,
Antora loads it without complaint, and then the build fails with `block.$!= is not a function` — 1.x
targets Asciidoctor.js 3 while Antora 3.1 bundles 2.x. The error names neither the package nor the
version, and the working pin is `0.18.1`. Both facts are now comments at the two places that install
it, because nothing else in the tree would have said so.

=== What the workflow does that `ci.yml` deliberately does not

`.github/workflows/docs.yml` is separate, on two grounds. Deploying needs `pages: write` and
`id-token: write`, and those have no business being held by the job that also decodes a signing
keystore. And the two checks are different questions: `ci.yml` runs `asciidoctor --failure-level=WARN`
over each file, which parses it; this one runs `antora --log-failure-level=warn`, which resolves the
whole site. An `xref:` to a page that does not exist is invisible to the first and fatal to the
second — verified by pointing one at a missing page and watching the build exit 1.

Kroki runs as two service containers, because the core image delegates Mermaid to a headless-browser
companion. The companion has no health check and the core answers `/health` well before it can draw
anything, so the readiness step *renders a diagram in a loop* rather than sleeping. It also sends
`Content-Type: text/plain` — the same trap as yesterday: without it curl form-encodes the body, Kroki
mangles it, and the failure reads as a broken diagram rather than a broken request.

=== State

Locally green from the rebuilt container: 22 pages, all 26 diagrams, no warnings.

The build half then proved itself sooner than expected: because the workflow's own path filter matches
the pull request that adds it, PR #107 ran it — green in 1m18s, Kroki rendering on the first attempt,
the same 22 pages CI produced as the container did. That is the half I had written off as unobservable
until merge, and it was observable one push earlier.

The deploy half followed on merge, and it worked first time: `deploy` green in 10 seconds, the site up
at https://drehtuer.github.io/podsilo[drehtuer.github.io/podsilo]. Checked against the published pages
rather than the job's own verdict — 20 pages in the sitemap, and `architecture.html`'s diagrams load as
site assets, which is what says `kroki-fetch-diagram` really did fetch them and left no reference to a
localhost only the builder can reach.

So the documents are now readable somewhere the `xref:` macros resolve, which is what the Antora move
cost three commits to buy. Nothing in §1 is left unverified for this pipeline.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (navbar) — deleting someone else's demo content

*Attempted:* remove the "Products" and "Services" dropdowns the published site had been carrying.

They came from the stock Antora UI bundle, which ships a navbar advertising Product A/B/C and Service
A/B/C, each one an `href="#"`. Nobody wrote them here and nobody had looked at the header closely
enough to notice — the site went live and the demo content went with it, which is the ordinary way a
default becomes a decision.

The fix is `ui.supplemental_files`: one file, `supplemental-ui/partials/header-content.hbs`, replacing
the bundle's copy. Worth recording because supplemental files *replace* rather than patch, so the
override has to carry the parts of upstream's header that are still wanted — the search field is in
there doing nothing, kept so that setting `SITE_SEARCH_PROVIDER` later still works. And since the
bundle is the rolling HEAD artifact, an override can silently drift from the file it overrides with no
warning at all. The comment at the top of the file says both.

The path filter in `docs.yml` needed `supplemental-ui/**` adding, or a change to the site's appearance
would not have rebuilt the site.

*Left as-is, deliberately:* the same navbar still has a "Home" link and a "Download" button, both also
`href="#"`. They were not what was asked for, and where they should point (the site root? the releases
page? nowhere?) is the author's call, not mine.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (security policy) — the document the site never mentioned

`SECURITY.md` was reachable from the README and from GitHub's Security tab, and from nowhere in the
documentation site. Now that the site is the reading surface, that is a gap: the file says how to
report a vulnerability privately, what is in scope, and which limitations are deliberate — all of it
addressed to a reader who is on the docs, not in the file tree.

Not fixed by converting it to a page. GitHub reads a security policy from `SECURITY.md` at the
repository root; move it into `docs/modules/ROOT/pages/` and the Security tab loses it, and copying it
would give the project two versions of a policy to keep in step. So it stays where it is and is linked
three ways: from the site nav (present on every page, `^` so it opens in a new tab, since it leaves the
site), from `index.adoc`'s list of documents with a note saying *why* it is the one document not
hosted here, and from `dev-environment.adoc`'s *See also* — the build and release path is in its
scope, and it is where the "device tests are not run in CI" claim is stated to an outside reader.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (brief) — CLAUDE.md becomes CLAUDE.adoc

The standing brief was the last Markdown document of any size in the repository, and the only one
outside the reach of the `asciidoctor --failure-level=WARN` check CI runs over every `.adoc`. Converted
with kramdoc, the same tool the documentation conversion used.

Claude Code loads a file named `CLAUDE.md` and nothing else, so `.claude/CLAUDE.md` stays as a stub
that imports the real file with `@CLAUDE.adoc`. Three details from the documentation decided how that
stub is written: a relative import resolves against *the importing file's* directory, not the working
directory; import parsing skips code spans, so a backticked `@path` is deliberately inert and the
import must be bare; and HTML comments in a `CLAUDE.md` are stripped before it reaches context, which
is where the maintainer note explaining all this lives, costing no tokens.

=== Checking a conversion rather than trusting it

kramdoc is good but not lossless, and this file is the one document whose exact wording is load-bearing
— it is read by an agent as instructions, not by a person as prose. So the check was mechanical: pull
every alphanumeric token out of both files in order and diff the two streams. That reduces the
comparison to *content*, ignoring every markup difference, and it found the one real loss immediately.

*Ordered lists lost their numbers.* Markdown writes `1.`, `2.`, `3.`; AsciiDoc's `.` marker
auto-numbers, so the rendered output is identical and the *source* is not. That matters here in a way
it would not for a page on the site: this file is consumed as raw text, so "§1 requirement 2" stops
being findable when the source says only `.`. Explicit numbers were restored to all 43 items, and the
proof that it changed nothing is that Asciidoctor renders both forms to byte-identical HTML — apart
from one improvement, the nested list under §7, which AsciiDoc styles a., b., c. by default and which
the Markdown had as 1–10.

After that, the token diff between the original and the finished `.adoc` is: the SPDX line, `md` →
`adoc` in the title, two document attributes, the paragraph explaining the split, and three
`line-through` tokens where GFM strikethrough became AsciiDoc's spelling. Nothing else moved.

*Unverified:* whether the import actually expands is not observable from inside this session — the
brief was already in context when it started. It needs a fresh session and `/context`, which is in the
stub's maintainer note.

'''

== 2026-08-23 (security page) — one source, two renderings

Yesterday the site linked *out* to `SECURITY.md` on GitHub. The author asked the obvious question:
why not convert it with kramdoc, as the brief had just been converted?

Because the two files are constrained differently, and the constraint is not in the file — it is in
who reads it. Claude Code has a documented `@import`, so a three-line `CLAUDE.md` stub genuinely
expands into the whole brief. GitHub has no include for `SECURITY.md`: the filename *is* the
integration, and the reader is a stranger who has found a vulnerability and clicked *Report a
vulnerability*. A stub there degrades the one page that must not be a signpost, and GitHub's own
documentation does not say which extensions it detects — so `SECURITY.adoc` is a guess, made on the
document least suited to guessing.

What the question was actually right about is that the policy should be *readable on the site*. The
way to have both is to keep one source and render it twice: `SECURITY.md` stays at the root for
GitHub, and `scripts/build-docs.sh` runs kramdoc over it into `docs/modules/ROOT/pages/security.adoc`
before Antora runs. The generated page is gitignored — a generated file in git is a file that drifts —
and carries a "do not edit" banner, because it lands in a directory of hand-written pages.

The build is now a script rather than an `antora` invocation, and that is not tidiness: running
`antora` alone leaves the nav pointing at a page that does not exist, which `--log-failure-level=warn`
turns into a failed build. One script means the local build and `docs.yml` cannot drift apart.

=== A dead link nobody had followed

Converting forced a look at the file's links, and `SECURITY.md`'s only in-repo document link —
`[third-party.adoc](third-party.adoc)` — had been *dead since the Antora restructure* moved that file
to `docs/modules/ROOT/pages/`. It had been broken on GitHub for three commits and nobody noticed,
because the check that would have caught it (`--failure-level=WARN`, then Antora's xref resolution)
only ever looked at `.adoc`. Both of the file's relative links are now absolute — one to the published
site, one to the file on GitHub — which is what they have to be for a document that is rendered in two
places.


== 2026-09-07 (coverage) — the measurement that reported nothing

*Attempted:* code coverage for the Tier 1 unit tests, uploaded to Codecov, with a percentage badge in
`README.adoc`. The author had already set the token as a repository secret.

*What worked, structurally.* Twelve modules split cleanly in two. The four pure-JVM ones take Gradle's
`jacoco` plugin and its `jacocoTestReport`; the eight Android ones take AGP's
`enableUnitTestCoverage = true`, which registers `createDebugUnitTestCoverageReport` itself. Neither
side needed a hand-written `JacocoReport` task, an `.exec` merge, or a percentage computed in Gradle —
which was the thing to avoid (CLAUDE.md §3). A root `coverage` task runs both, and CI collects the
twelve XML reports with `find` rather than a written-down list, so a thirteenth module is covered
without editing the workflow. `decisions/0028` records the shape.

*What did not work, and is the actual content of this entry.* The first green run reported *28.4 %*,
and 28 % is a perfectly believable number for an Android project — which is exactly why it was nearly
accepted. Two rows in the per-module breakdown did not fit: `:core:database` at *0 % of 2629 lines*,
and `:core:ui` at 7.6 %. Both are tested exclusively through Robolectric. `:core:datastore`, which
uses none, sat at a sensible 60 %.

Reading the raw execution data settled it in one command: `:core:database`'s 91 KB `.exec` contained
*no class name from this project at all* — the classes it had recorded were Gradle's, ASM's, and
Robolectric's own. Not a discarded mismatch; the agent had never seen them. Robolectric loads the
class under test in a sandbox class loader that defines it with no code-source location, and JaCoCo
skips no-location classes by default. One flag (`isIncludeNoLocationClasses`, plus a mandatory
`jdk.internal.*` exclusion, or the test JVM dies on startup) took the same unchanged test suite from
28.4 % to *77.2 %*.

*And then it failed a second time, silently.* The flag was first written as a bare
`tasks.withType<Test>().configureEach` in the root `subprojects { }` block, the whole suite was re-run,
and every number came back byte-identical. That block is evaluated while the root project is, before
any module; the `jacoco` plugin adds the extension from its own `withType(Test)` action; actions run
at realisation time in registration order. Ours ran first, found no extension, and the null-safe call
swallowed it. Hanging it off `pluginManager.withPlugin("jacoco")` fixed it. Two rounds of "the number
did not move" for two different reasons, neither of which produced an error.

*Lesson, and it is the same one this project keeps paying for:* a measurement that silently reports
nothing is indistinguishable from a measurement that reports a low number, and the plausible one is
more dangerous than a crash. The habit that caught it was not suspicion of Robolectric — it was
printing the per-module breakdown instead of the total. `:core:database` at *exactly* 0.0 % of 2629
lines is not a coverage result; it is an absence of data wearing one. A single aggregate figure would
have hidden it perfectly, which is worth remembering the next time one number looks like enough.

*Deliberately not done:* no coverage threshold and no failing status check. Both Codecov statuses are
`informational`. CLAUDE.md §7 ranks what matters — reconciliation first, Compose smoke tests last —
and one repository-wide percentage cannot express that ranking, so a gate on it would fail pull
requests for reasons nobody intends to act on. CI does fail on one thing: finding no reports to
upload, because Codecov accepts an upload of nothing and leaves the badge showing last week's number.

*Verified:* `./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt coverage` green in the dev container, 77.2 % line coverage
across the twelve modules. The Codecov upload itself is *unverified* — it can only run on GitHub
Actions, so the first push is what proves the token, the action version and the report paths.


== 2026-09-12 (repo rename) — `drehtuer/podsilo` became `drehtuer/podsiloApp`

*Attempted:* the author renamed the GitHub repository to `podsiloApp`; adapt every reference to the
new slug.

*What the rename does and does not touch.* The distinction that made this a five-minute job rather
than a sweep through 12 modules: *the project is still called Podsilo.* Only the repository slug
changed. So the Kotlin package `net.drehtuer.podsilo`, the Antora component `podsilo::`, the APK and
artifact names (`podsilo-*.apk`, `podsilo-apks`), the `PODSILO_*` CI secrets, the on-device database
file, the `run-podsilo` skill and the `PODSILO_REPOSITORY_URL` constant's *name* all stay exactly as
they were. What changed is a URL prefix, in three shapes — `github.com/drehtuer/podsilo`,
`codecov.io/gh/drehtuer/podsilo` and the Pages host `drehtuer.github.io/podsilo` — which is why the
rewrite was anchored on those prefixes with a `(?!App)` guard rather than on the word "podsilo".
A blind `podsilo` → `podsiloApp` substitution would have renamed the package.

Eleven URLs across six files: the five README badges and their prose, two links in `SECURITY.md`, the
Antora playbook's `site.url`, the docs-site index, the clone command in `dev-environment.adoc` (and
the `cd` after it), and the About screen's source-code pointer. `git remote set-url` too. Note
`SECURITY.md`'s third-party link is now `drehtuer.github.io/podsiloApp/podsilo/third-party.html` —
the second segment is the Antora component name, not a missed replacement.

*Deliberately not changed.* Two things:

* `journal.adoc`'s 2026-08-30 entry quotes the About screen as it read that day, old URL and all.
Section 9 of this file says a log edited to match the present is not a log; GitHub redirects the old
slug permanently, so the citation still resolves.
* `.devcontainer/devcontainer.json` still mounts the tree at `/workspaces/podsilo`. That is a path
inside the container, bound from `+${localWorkspaceFolder}+` whatever the host directory is called, and
changing it would orphan the existing container and its named volumes for no gain.

*The redirect is a grace period, not a fix.* GitHub forwards the old slug until someone creates a new
repository at `drehtuer/podsilo`, at which point every unfixed link silently points at a stranger's
project. The badge SVGs would have been the visible symptom — they fetch by slug and would have kept
rendering, from the wrong repository.

*Verified:* `./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green in the dev container (the host has no Android
SDK — the run needs the `vsc-podsilo-*` image with the `podsilo-android-sdk` and `podsilo-gradle`
volumes), plus `yamllint .` and the `asciidoctor --failure-level=WARN` sweep over every `.adoc`. The
two yamllint line-length warnings in `ci.yml` predate this change. *Unverified:* the badges and the
Pages URL, which can only be confirmed once this lands on `main` and the site redeploys.


== 2026-09-12 (SonarQube) — the same reports, read by something that answers two questions

*Attempted:* replace Codecov with SonarQube Cloud for coverage; delete the Codecov configuration and
badge; add separate line and branch coverage badges. `SONAR_TOKEN` was already in the repository
secrets.

*The measurement did not move, and that was the point.* `./gradlew coverage` still writes the same
twelve JaCoCo XML reports by the same two mechanisms, and the Robolectric `isIncludeNoLocationClasses`
fix from 2026-09-07 is untouched. All that changed is the consumer: `sonar-project.properties` names
the two report globs and the `SonarSource/sonarqube-scan-action` step replaces the `find`-and-upload
pair. `decisions/0028` is amended rather than superseded, because its actual decision — coverage is
measured, not enforced — survives the vendor change intact.

*Two lookups worth recording, because both would have been easy to get wrong by assuming.*

`gh secret list` showed `SONAR_TOKEN` and *no* `SONAR_HOST_URL`, which is what settles SonarQube Cloud
over a self-hosted server without having to ask. And `api/components/search?organization=drehtuer`
returned the project already created, as `drehtuer_podsiloApp`, answering anonymously — which is the
other fact the badges depend on, since a private project would not serve its measures to shields.io.
Two `curl`s in place of two questions.

*The badge endpoint does not do what was asked, and the check for that was cheap.* SonarQube's own
`api/project_badges/measure` looks like the obvious way to render "`line coverage`" and "`branch
coverage`". It is not: `api/webservices/list` gives its `metric` parameter as a fixed allow-list of
fourteen values, and while `coverage` is on it, `line_coverage` and `branch_coverage` are not. Asking
the API to describe itself took one command and replaced a guess that would have shipped two badges
rendering an error image. The two are shields.io `dynamic/json` badges against
`api/measures/component` instead — the same shape a sibling repository of the author's already uses,
so this is a pattern being reused rather than invented (CLAUDE.md §3).

*Why two numbers and not one.* Line coverage says a line ran; branch coverage says both sides of a
decision ran. For `:core:sync` — the module §7 ranks first — a suite that walks the happy path of
every reconciliation rule and never takes a conflict branch is exactly the failure this project
should be able to see, and SonarQube's combined `coverage` metric averages it away. That is the same
lesson as 2026-09-07's, one level up: *an aggregate hides the thing you built the measurement to
find.* A single figure nearly hid `:core:database` at 0 %; a single figure would also hide untested
branches behind well-covered lines.

*The one piece of setup that is not a file.* SonarQube's automatic analysis and its CI scanner are
mutually exclusive — with autoscan on, the scan CI runs is rejected outright — and autoscan is also
incapable of reading a coverage report, so it had to go off. The author switched it off in the web
UI; `api/settings/values?keys=sonar.autoscan.enabled` confirms `false` against an instance default of
`true`. That is a dependency of the build that no checkout can show you, so it is written into
`sonar-project.properties` along with the command that checks it. The habit worth keeping: when a
green build depends on a setting in someone else's web UI, the repository should at least be able to
*tell you the setting exists*.

*Two things deliberately not adopted, both of which the tool offers and neither of which was asked
for.* No `-Dsonar.qualitygate.wait=true`, so a red gate cannot fail CI — turning it on would have
reversed `decisions/0028` as a side effect of changing vendors. And no `sonar.coverage.exclusions`:
excluding anything now would move the number away from the 77.2 % Codecov last reported for a reason
that has nothing to do with the tests, which ruins the only thing the badge is for. Both are noted in
the ADR so that adopting either later is a decision rather than a drift.

*Verified:* `./gradlew ktlintCheck detekt test` green in the dev container, plus `yamllint .`, the
`asciidoctor --failure-level=WARN` sweep, and a check that the `.properties` file's report globs match
files that `coverage` actually produces. The shields.io badge URLs were confirmed to render real
numbers against the author's already-analysed sibling project. *Unverified, and it can only be
verified by pushing:* the scan itself. Podsilo's SonarCloud project has no analysis yet
(`measures: []`), so the first CI run is what proves the token, the action, the report paths and the
badges at once — until then both badges will render an error, which is expected and not a mistake.

*Left for the author:* the now-unused `CODECOV_TOKEN` secret. Deleting a secret is not something to do
on someone's behalf.


== 2026-09-12 (signing) — one folder for the keys, and a debug key that is the same everywhere

*Attempted:* move the release keystore and its credentials into a `keystore/` directory, ignore that
directory, and add a signing key for the debug build.

*The move is the boring half, and it had exactly one way to go wrong quietly.* `keystore.properties`
is read by `app/build.gradle.kts` at a hard-coded relative path, and `storeFile` inside it is another
relative path; get either wrong and the build does not fail — it logs at `info` and produces an
*unsigned* release APK, because building unsigned rather than failing is the deliberate behaviour
when no key is configured. A silent downgrade, in other words, of the same family as the Codecov
upload that accepted nothing five days ago. So the check was not "`does it build`" but
`apksigner verify --print-certs` on the actual artifact: `CN=Podsilo` still on the release APK, which
is the only evidence that the path change landed.

*The debug key is the half worth having.* AGP signs debug builds with `~/.android/debug.keystore`,
which is generated *per machine* — so two debug APKs of the same commit, built in the dev container
and on the host, can never replace one another; the install fails with
`INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE` and the only way through is an uninstall that takes the ledger,
the login and the SAF grant with it. §10 had already documented that as a one-way door, diagnosed on
the Pixel 10a. A key kept with the project removes it between any two machines that hold the key.

It uses Android's own debug convention — alias `androiddebugkey`, password `android` — and the
documentation says in as many words that *this key is not a secret*. That matters because it lives
under an ignored directory next to a key that very much is, and the next person to look will
reasonably assume both are sensitive. Writing down which one is not is cheaper than having someone
treat a throwaway as irreplaceable.

*One Kotlin trap worth recording.* `getByName("debug") { storeFile = debugStoreFile }` does not
compile: the release block above declares `val storeFile`, which shadows the receiver's property
inside the lambda, and the error is `'val' cannot be reassigned` pointing at a line that is not
assigning to a `val` at all. `this.storeFile` fixes it — which is exactly why the release config had
already qualified its own. A comment now says so in both places.

*Two consequences, one paid immediately and one declined.*

* *The first debug install after this is a forced uninstall*, on every device already holding a debug
build — including the Tier 2 emulator. It is paid once, and it is the point. `dev-environment.adoc`
§10 carries the warning next to the `keytool` invocation rather than only here.
* *CI is deliberately not given the debug key.* `keystore/` is ignored, so a runner falls back to
AGP's generated keystore exactly as before, and a CI-built debug APK remains a one-way door against
every local build. Carrying the `.jks` as a fourth secret would fix that, but the value of a shared
debug key is that *your own* machines agree, and §10 already tells you not to sideload the debug APK
from a release page.

*Noted, not built:* `run-podsilo`'s `driver.py` installs with `adb install -r -t` and has no fallback
for a signature mismatch, so the skill will fail once on any emulator holding an older debug build.
The fix is small but deletes app data, which makes it a decision rather than a tidy-up — it went to
`backlog.adoc` (CLAUDE.md §9) instead of being slipped in alongside a signing change.

*No ADR for this one.* `dev-environment.adoc` §10 now carries the layout, both `keytool`
invocations, the not-a-secret statement, the uninstall warning and the reason CI is excluded. A
`decisions/` entry would have restated it a second time in a second place, which is the drift those
files exist to prevent.

*Verified:* `ktlintCheck detekt assembleDebug assembleRelease` green in the dev container, and both
APKs checked with `apksigner verify --print-certs` — debug now `CN=Podsilo Debug` where it used to be
`CN=Android Debug`, release still `CN=Podsilo`. `git check-ignore` confirms all three files under
`keystore/` are ignored by the new directory rule. *Unverified:* an actual install over an existing
debug build — the uninstall-first requirement above is inferred from Android's documented signature
rule and from this project's own 2026-08-09 diagnosis, not observed today.

*Reversed the same day, at the author's request.* The paragraph above declines to give CI the debug
key; the author asked for signed debug builds on CI and added the four `PODSILO_DEBUG_*` secrets, so
the workflow now decodes the debug keystore beside the release one and passes both to
`assembleDebug assembleRelease`. The reasoning that was declined was never that it would not work --
it was that the benefit looked narrow. It is not: it retires the "`one-way door`" that
`dev-environment.adoc` §10 has carried since 2026-08-09, because the debug APK on a release page
now upgrades in place from a local build and vice versa. That section is rewritten rather than
deleted, since it is still exactly what happens if the secret goes missing or a fork builds without
it.

Two details worth keeping. *The two keys fail differently and neither fails the build* — a missing
release key yields an unsigned APK that cannot be installed, a missing debug key falls back to AGP's
generated one and installs fine — so the decode steps look alike but their warnings say different
things. And *three of the four new secrets are not secret*: the alias and both passwords are the
published Android convention. They are stored as secrets for symmetry with the release key, which is
defensible, but it makes a secret list where two entries called `…KEY_PASSWORD` have completely
different consequences if leaked. The documented value is therefore written out in full in §10's
table, so the distinction is visible to whoever reads it next rather than living in one person's
memory.


== 2026-09-12 (docs build) — the lint was green and the site was broken

*What happened:* the repo-rename journal entry written earlier today explains that
`.devcontainer/devcontainer.json` binds the workspace from a dollar-brace VS Code variable. Written
literally inside backticks, AsciiDoc reads that as an attribute reference. Antora is configured to
warn on a missing attribute and `scripts/build-docs.sh` runs it with `--log-failure-level=warn`, so
the Docs workflow failed on the first push to `main` after the merge.

*The part worth recording is not the escaping — it is that every check I ran was green.*
`git ls-files -z "*.adoc" | xargs -0 asciidoctor --failure-level=WARN` passed, and that is the
command this repository documents as the AsciiDoc check. It passes because the Ruby CLI defaults to
`attribute-missing=skip`, while Antora's JavaScript Asciidoctor is configured to warn. Two engines,
two settings, one of them not the one CI runs. Adding `-a attribute-missing=warn` to the Ruby command
does not close the gap either — tried, and it still exited 0 on the same file.

So the only check that sees this class is the site build itself. It was available the whole time --
`./scripts/build-docs.sh`, with Kroki up in compose -- and I did not run it because the change "`was
only a journal entry`". That is the reasoning to distrust: a journal entry is a page in the site like
any other.

*Verified by reproduction, not assumption.* With the fix, `build-docs.sh` exits 0; with the same
file stashed back to its broken state, it exits 1 and names `+skipping reference to missing
attribute: localworkspacefolder+`. Both directions run, because "`the build passes now`" on its own
does not establish that the edit is what changed it.

*Noted, not built:* `docs.yml` triggers on `push: branches: [main]` only, so the site build first
runs *after* a merge -- which is precisely how this reached `main` with every pull-request check
green. Adding `pull_request:` is three lines, but `deploy` would then need guarding with
`if: github.event_name == 'push'`, and getting that wrong either publishes from a branch or stops
publishing altogether. That belongs in its own change, so it went to `backlog.adoc`.
`dev-environment.adoc` now carries a caution next to the asciidoctor command saying plainly that it
is a syntax check and not a site check.


== 2026-09-12 (release) — 0.7.1, and what a release means when nothing shipped

*Attempted:* cut v0.7.1.

*The bump itself is one line* -- `versionName` in `app/build.gradle.kts` is the only place a human
chooses a number, and `versionCode` is `git rev-list --count HEAD`, so nothing else needs editing.
This is the first release where the README is not also edited: the status block that every previous
bump rewrote was removed earlier today, deliberately, because release-specific prose in a file that
describes the project goes stale the moment a release does. The Release badge points at the current
one instead. Do not reintroduce it at the next bump.

*What is honestly in it.* Almost everything done since v0.7.0 does not reach the APK -- the repository
rename, the Codecov-to-SonarQube switch, the `keystore/` move, the debug signing key, four README
edits. Checking rather than assuming: `git diff v0.7.0..main -- '*/src/**'` reports 181 files and
*545 insertions against 545 deletions*, and that symmetry is the tell. It is a 1:1 substitution --
872 of those lines are `docs/UI.md` becoming `UI.adoc` and similar, in comments and KDoc, from the
AsciiDoc conversion. Filtering the comment lines out leaves exactly two real changes: a test
assertion message, and `PODSILO_REPOSITORY_URL`.

So the release is: *the About screen's "`Source code`" link now points at the renamed repository*
(it would have redirected until someone else claimed the old slug), plus Coil 3.5.0 → 3.6.2,
navigation-compose 2.9.8 → 2.10.0 and OkHttp 5.4.0 → 5.5.0, which do ship. That is a patch release
and is described as one. The temptation with a fortnight of visible activity behind it is to write
release notes about the activity; the notes describe what a user gets, which is one fixed link and
three dependency bumps.

*The one behaviour change that is not in the APK at all:* the debug APK attached to the release is
now signed with the project debug key rather than the runner's generated one, so for the first time
it can be upgraded in place by a locally built debug build. `dev-environment.adoc` §10 still says
sideload the release APK -- that advice is unchanged, because debug and release remain different keys.

*Verified:* `ktlintCheck detekt test` green, and `assembleRelease` produces `podsilo-0.7.1.apk`
signed `CN=Podsilo` -- the name is derived from `versionName`, so building it is what proves the bump
took. *Unverified:* nothing has been installed on a phone from this build; the app code is unchanged
from v0.7.0 apart from the one string, which is why that is acceptable here and would not be for a
feature release.


== 2026-09-13 — Some podcasts never stayed played, and the server was answering 200 while storing nothing

*Attempted:* diagnose why episodes on a few podcasts kept coming back as new -- in Podsilo, and in
RePod, which is what made it interesting.

*The report named the shape of the answer before I started looking.* "`Also visible in the Nextcloud
podcast app`" rules out reconciliation here: two independent clients reading the same log and both
seeing nothing means the log has nothing in it. And "`feeds on supercast with a user specific hash`"
says the feeds differ from the working ones in exactly one measurable way. So the question was never
"`what is wrong with sync`" but "`what about this feed does the server not like`".

Measuring it took one `curl`: every enclosure URL in that feed is *516--517 characters*, because
Supercast signs each one with a per-subscriber token. `gpodder_episode_action.episode` is
`VARCHAR(500)`, and has been since the table was created in 2021.

*The part that makes it a silent failure rather than an error* is eleven lines of
`EpisodeActionSaver::saveEpisodeActions`: it catches every `OCP\DB\Exception`, asks whether the
reason was a unique-constraint violation, and -- when it was not, as "`Data too long for column`"
is not -- falls out of the `catch` with no rethrow and no log. `EpisodeActionController::create`
never reads what the saver returned and answers `200 {"timestamp": ...}` either way. So Podsilo
posted, read a 2xx, set `syncedToServer = true`, and the server held nothing. *Nothing anywhere said
so* -- not in the app, not in the server's log.

*It is known upstream and was abandoned.* `thrillfall/nextcloud-gpodder#81` carries the identical
diagnosis from a user hitting it on Acast at 612 characters, and the maintainer's reply --
"`Ooooooooooohhhhhh. Thanks!. Well increase the length then`" -- is from 2022. 3.17.0 still creates
the column at 500. Only `gpodder_subscriptions.url` was ever widened, which is precisely why
*subscribing* to such a feed works and marking its episodes played does not. Finding that comment
was worth more than the hour it would have taken to reason it out alone, and it is the second time
on this project that reading the server's issue tracker beat reasoning from its source.

*Two fixes, in two repositories, and only one of them is Podsilo's.* The server half is a schema
correction in the author's Ansible `cloud` role -- widen the three columns to 1000, re-asserted on
every run so an app update that recreates the column at 500 is corrected rather than quietly
resuming the loss.

*I got half of that wrong, and the author caught it before it shipped.* I also rebuilt the two unique
indexes over a 500-character prefix first, reasoning that InnoDB caps an index key at 3072 bytes and
a full utf8mb4 `VARCHAR(1000)` is 4000. The reasoning is real and the conclusion did not follow. The
author tested it on `mariadb:10.11` -- the version `carcosa` runs -- and the statements are *a
no-op*: at that point the columns still _are_ 500, so the prefixes normalise away and
`SHOW CREATE TABLE` comes back byte-identical. Worse, the widen they were supposed to enable needs
no help at all, because MariaDB has implemented an over-cap unique index as a hash over the whole
value since 10.4 and converts it in place.

The lesson is specific rather than "`test more`". I copied `Version0006`'s *statements* while
reading it as a sequence, and it is not one: Doctrine emits an index against the final schema, not
in written order, which is why the subscriptions table really does end up `(url(500), user_id)` and
mine would not have. Copying a migration's output is not the same as copying a migration. The bit I
could have checked cheaply -- does `SHOW CREATE TABLE` differ afterwards -- I did not, because the
statements *looked* like they did something.

*The Podsilo half is the one that generalises.* The author asked for the app to say when the server
did not take the state, which is a better request than the one I would have built: my first instinct
was a length check against 500, and that is a client hardcoding a server constant that the same
change was about to make wrong. Verifying instead -- "`did what I sent come back?`" -- asks the
question that actually matters and survives the fix.

*It costs no extra request, which is the only reason it is worth doing on every pass.* The server
selects `WHERE timestamp_epoch > :since` on the *client-authored* timestamp inside each action --
the value this device wrote. So for our own rows the comparison is between two numbers on the same
clock, and anything we sent whose `actionedAt` is later than the `since` we asked for must come back
if the server kept it. The pull that already follows the push in the same pass is the proof.

*The asymmetry was designed in rather than discovered.* A device whose clock runs behind the
server's fails the `askedSince` test and is simply not checked, so skew can only ever silence this,
never make it report a loss that did not happen. Same for the truncating backend -- a non-strict
MySQL stores the first 500 characters rather than refusing the row, and the action comes back keyed
by its `guid`, which still matches. Both have a test, because both are the ways a check like this
turns into a boy who cried wolf.

*What is not done, deliberately:* the rows are not put back in the outbox. Re-sending the same
oversized URL fails identically for ever, so a retrying outbox would be a permanent stuck count and a
permanent repeat of the same log entry. The entry names the repair instead -- S4's _Send this
device's state to Nextcloud_, once the column can hold them.

*Verified:* `:core:sync` ktlintCheck, detekt and test green -- 84 tests, 7 of them new. The Ansible
change is `yamllint --strict` and `ansible-lint --profile production` clean.

*Since corrected on the other side:* the author proved the `ALTER`s against a faithful copy of the
table on `mariadb:10.11` -- a 516-character insert raises the 1406 the app swallows, the three
``MODIFY``s make the same insert succeed, and repeating it still raises 1062, so the uniqueness
constraint is intact rather than traded away. That is the half of this that is now demonstrated
rather than argued.

*Still unverified, and worth stating plainly:* the Podsilo check
has been proven against fakes and not against an instance that actually drops an action;
`opodsync`, the test server in `.devcontainer`, *cannot* reproduce the bug at all, because SQLite
ignores a declared `VARCHAR` width. The honest end-to-end proof is: apply the role, force-push the
affected podcast's decisions, and confirm they appear in RePod.

*Toolchain note for the next session:* this ran on the WSL2 host rather than in the dev container,
which has no JDK and no Android SDK, so `./gradlew test` cannot run there as written. A Temurin 17
in a scratch directory was enough for the pure-JVM modules, which is all this change touches. The
Android modules were not built. Run the full `ktlintCheck detekt test` in the container before
taking the green above as the whole story.
